- **Score**: 7/10

### Decoding Instructional Dialogue: Human-AI Collaborative Analysis of Teacher Use of AI Tool at Scale
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.17985v1)
- **Authors**: Alex Liu, Lief Esbenshade, Shawon Sarkar, Victor Tian, Zachary Zhang, Kevin He, Min Sun
- **Abstract**: The integration of large language models (LLMs) into educational tools has the potential to substantially impact how teachers plan instruction, support diverse learners, and engage in professional reflection. Yet little is known about how educators actually use these tools in practice and how their interactions with AI can be meaningfully studied at scale. This paper presents a human-AI collaborative methodology for large-scale qualitative analysis of over 140,000 educator-AI messages drawn from a generative AI platform used by K-12 teachers. Through a four-phase coding pipeline, we combined inductive theme discovery, codebook development, structured annotation, and model benchmarking to examine patterns of educator engagement and evaluate the performance of LLMs in qualitative coding tasks. We developed a hierarchical codebook aligned with established teacher evaluation frameworks, capturing educators' instructional goals, contextual needs, and pedagogical strategies. Our findings demonstrate that LLMs, particularly Claude 3.5 Haiku, can reliably support theme identification, extend human recognition in complex scenarios, and outperform open-weight models in both accuracy and structural reliability. The analysis also reveals substantive patterns in how educators inquire AI to enhance instructional practices (79.7 percent of total conversations), create or adapt content (76.1 percent), support assessment and feedback loop (46.9 percent), attend to student needs for tailored instruction (43.3 percent), and assist other professional responsibilities (34.2 percent), highlighting emerging AI-related competencies that have direct implications for teacher preparation and professional development. This study offers a scalable, transparent model for AI-augmented qualitative research and provides foundational insights into the evolving role of generative AI in educational practice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a human-AI collaborative methodology for analyzing teacher-AI dialogues at scale. It examines over 140,000 educator-AI messages from a generative AI platform used by K-12 teachers.  The researchers used a four-phase coding pipeline involving inductive theme discovery, codebook development, structured annotation, and model benchmarking.  They developed a hierarchical codebook aligned with teacher evaluation frameworks.  The study evaluates LLM performance in qualitative coding tasks, finding that models like Claude 3.5 Haiku can reliably support theme identification and extend human capabilities. The analysis also reveals patterns in how educators use AI to enhance instructional practices, create content, support assessment, attend to student needs, and assist with other professional responsibilities. The paper argues for a scalable, transparent model for AI-augmented qualitative research and provides insights into the evolving role of generative AI in education.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scalability:** The paper addresses a key challenge in qualitative research: analyzing large datasets. The methodology provides a pathway for handling large volumes of textual data that would be impractical to analyze manually.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The study incorporates elements of grounded theory, teacher evaluation frameworks, and structured prompt engineering to ensure the validity and interpretability of the findings.  The multi-phase approach provides a level of structure and transparency often lacking in initial AI-assisted qualitative studies.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The findings provide valuable insights into how teachers are actually using AI tools and what types of support they are seeking.  This has direct implications for the design of AI tools and teacher professional development.
    *   **Detailed Comparison:** The comparison of different LLMs, including open-source models, and human coders provides a nuanced assessment of their capabilities and limitations.  This is crucial for making informed decisions about which models to use in different research contexts.
    *   **Explicit Focus on Human-AI Collaboration:** The paper emphasizes the complementary roles of humans and AI, rather than viewing AI as a replacement for human researchers.  This aligns with best practices in responsible AI research.
    *   **Careful Consideration of Limitations:** The authors thoroughly acknowledge the study's limitations, including interpretive ambiguity, the difficulty of human annotation at scale, prompt sensitivity, and the limited scope of the LLM comparison. This improves the credibility of the paper and provides suggestions for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Interpretive Bias:** As acknowledged by the authors, the coding process inevitably involves interpretive bias. While the use of multiple coders and iterative refinement helps to mitigate this bias, it is still a potential concern. The specific choices made in prompt design can significantly influence the results.
    *   **Limited Contextual Data:** While the platform provides some contextual data (e.g., grade level, subject area), it lacks detailed information about the specific instructional contexts in which teachers are using AI. This limits the ability to draw firm conclusions about the effectiveness of AI in different settings.
    *   **Rapidly Evolving Landscape:** The field of LLMs is rapidly evolving. The findings of this study may not be generalizable to future versions of the models or to entirely new models. The model selection might have been more diverse by including smaller open-weight models trained for longer for domain expertise.
    *   **Potential for Over-Reliance on AI:** There is a risk that researchers might become overly reliant on AI and fail to critically evaluate the model's outputs. This is mitigated in the present study by the careful attention to human verification and refinement, but it is a general concern in AI-assisted research.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The study contributes to the growing body of research on AI-assisted qualitative analysis by providing a detailed and validated methodology.
    *   It extends previous work by focusing on a specific application domain (teacher-AI dialogues) and by conducting a large-scale empirical analysis.
    *   The findings provide valuable insights into how teachers are using AI tools and what types of support they are seeking.
    *   The comparison of different LLMs and human coders provides a more nuanced assessment of their capabilities and limitations.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of AI in education and qualitative research methodologies. The rigorous methodology, practical relevance, and detailed comparison of models are all strengths. While the limitations are acknowledged and partly addressed, the rapidly evolving AI landscape needs consideration. The framework detailed in this paper can be considered a significant step toward responsible integration of AI in complex qualitative research, in education and potentially in other domains.
However, it is also essential to acknowledge the field's rapid advancements and to continue updating such evaluations based on new information. This study offers an analysis methodology and real-world usage which can serve as a reference for future models and frameworks.

Score: 8
Rationale: Despite inherent limitations of conducting AI-assisted research in a rapidly changing area, the study is well-executed and provides valuable insights that advance the field. The thoroughness, and replicable framework makes it a significant contribution deserving of a high score.

### Unlock the Potential of Fine-grained LLM Serving via Dynamic Module Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18006v1)
- **Authors**: Jingfeng Wu, Yiyuan He, Minxian Xu, Xitong Gao, Kejiang Ye, Chengzhong Xu
- **Abstract**: The rise of large language models (LLMs) has created new opportunities across various fields but has also introduced significant challenges in resource management. Current LLM serving systems face a fundamental tension: balancing serving demands with limited resources while adapting to unpredictable traffic patterns. Static deployments lead to suboptimal resource utilization and performance degradation under dynamic workloads. Furthermore, the high cost of adjusting instances hinders dynamic scaling, limiting the true potential of efficient LLM serving. To address this, we propose CoCoServe, an elastic system that facilitates dynamic and fine-grained scaling. Its key innovation lies in the module-level operations for the replication and migration of LLM modules, such as decoder layers and projections. Through a comprehensive analysis of the trade-offs associated with these operations, we develop an auto-scaling mechanism that dynamically regulates module-level resource allocation and performance optimization, enabling a more cost-effective deployment of LLMs. Our evaluation demonstrates that the scaling operations employed by CoCoServe exhibit excellent scalability and can reduce costs by 46% while maintaining availability. Compared to state-of-the-art LLM serving systems (e.g., Hugging Face Transformers and vLLM), our approach reduces latency by 14%-75% and achieves 1.16x-4x throughput on average across different model sizes and workloads.
- **Summary**: This paper proposes CoCoServe, an elastic system for fine-grained LLM serving via dynamic module scaling. The core idea is to enable module-level replication and migration of LLM components (e.g., decoder layers, projections) rather than replicating/migrating entire LLM instances. The paper analyzes the trade-offs of these operations and develops an auto-scaling mechanism for resource allocation and performance optimization. Experiments demonstrate that CoCoServe can reduce costs, latency, and improve throughput compared to existing LLM serving systems like Hugging Face Transformers and vLLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of module-level scaling in LLM serving is somewhat novel. While instance-level dynamic scaling has been explored, the fine-grained control offered by module-level scaling has the potential to optimize resource utilization more efficiently. The paper contributes by formally defining module-level scaling operations (replication and migration), analyzing their trade-offs, and designing an auto-scaling mechanism based on these operations. However, the building blocks, particularly hook-based replication/migration implementation, and autoscaling policies based on thresholding aren't revolutionary.

**Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in its ability to improve the cost-effectiveness and performance of LLM serving. Reducing latency and increasing throughput are crucial for MaaS providers and end-users.  The demonstrated improvements over existing systems, particularly in memory utilization and reduced OOM rates, are significant. The authors provide concrete experimental results to support these claims, including ablation studies of the proposed scaling operations. However, the complexity of the auto-scaling algorithm and its reliance on various thresholds might make it challenging to deploy and tune in real-world scenarios. Also, the cost model provided is overly simplified.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of resource management in LLM serving and the limitations of existing instance-level scaling solutions.
*   **Well-Defined Module-Level Operations:** The concepts of module replication and migration are well-defined, and the paper analyzes their trade-offs in terms of performance and resource usage.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, comparing CoCoServe against state-of-the-art systems under various workloads and model sizes.  The results show significant improvements in latency, throughput, and memory utilization.
*   **Open Sourced code:** The availability of the code adds to the reproducibility and impact of this work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Implementation Complexity:** The implementation of the auto-scaling mechanism and the hook-based data transfer might be complex and challenging to deploy in real-world scenarios. The overhead is kept minimal; the system is still complex.
*   **Threshold Tuning:** The auto-scaling mechanism relies on several thresholds (Tup, Tdown), which need to be tuned for different workloads and deployment environments. This could make the system less robust and require significant manual intervention.
*   **Oversimplified Cost Model:** The speedup/cost model used in the auto-scaling algorithm appears simplified and potentially does not accurately represent the real-world performance of different replication strategies in heterogeneous environments. The communication cost is treated as a constant across layers.
*   **Limited Heterogeneous Evaluation:** Evaluation appears focused on homogenous GPU environments. A more extensive evaluation in heterogeneous environments is needed to fully validate the benefits of CoCoServe's module-level scaling.
*   **Lack of comparison with specialized frameworks:** While comparisons with vLLM and HFT are useful, there is no discussion/comparison with frameworks that specialize in dynamic model management, which could be relevant.

**Justification for Score:**

CoCoServe presents a novel and valuable contribution to the field of LLM serving by introducing the concept of module-level scaling. While the individual components (replication, migration, auto-scaling) are not entirely groundbreaking, their combination and application at the module level demonstrate the potential for improved resource utilization and performance. The experimental results are compelling, showing significant advantages over existing systems. However, the implementation complexity, threshold tuning requirements, simplified cost model, and a need for more evaluations in heterogenous environments limit the overall impact and practicality of the system.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cloud Native System for LLM Inference Serving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18007v1)
- **Authors**: Minxian Xu, Junhan Liao, Jingfeng Wu, Yiyuan He, Kejiang Ye, Chengzhong Xu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are revolutionizing numerous industries, but their substantial computational demands create challenges for efficient deployment, particularly in cloud environments. Traditional approaches to inference serving often struggle with resource inefficiencies, leading to high operational costs, latency issues, and limited scalability. This article explores how Cloud Native technologies, such as containerization, microservices, and dynamic scheduling, can fundamentally improve LLM inference serving. By leveraging these technologies, we demonstrate how a Cloud Native system enables more efficient resource allocation, reduces latency, and enhances throughput in high-demand scenarios. Through real-world evaluations using Kubernetes-based autoscaling, we show that Cloud Native architectures can dynamically adapt to workload fluctuations, mitigating performance bottlenecks while optimizing LLM inference serving performance. This discussion provides a broader perspective on how Cloud Native frameworks could reshape the future of scalable LLM inference serving, offering key insights for researchers, practitioners, and industry leaders in cloud computing and artificial intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Cloud Native System for LLM Inference Serving" addresses the challenges of deploying and scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) for inference in cloud environments. It argues that traditional inference serving approaches struggle with resource inefficiencies, high latency, and limited scalability due to the computational demands of LLMs.  The paper proposes a Cloud Native architecture, leveraging technologies like containerization, microservices, and dynamic scheduling, to improve resource allocation, reduce latency, and enhance throughput. The authors demonstrate through real-world evaluations using Kubernetes-based autoscaling that their approach can dynamically adapt to workload fluctuations, mitigating performance bottlenecks and optimizing LLM inference serving performance. The paper advocates for a shift towards Cloud Native frameworks to reshape the future of scalable LLM inference serving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a highly relevant and pressing problem. Efficient LLM inference serving is crucial for widespread adoption and real-world applications of these models. The cost and latency of LLM inference are key bottlenecks hindering accessibility and deployment.
    *   **Clear Explanation of Challenges:** The paper provides a well-articulated explanation of the challenges in optimizing LLM inference serving, including workload variability, resource imbalances, rigid scheduling, and inaccurate resource profiling.
    *   **Cloud Native Solution Proposal:** The proposal to leverage Cloud Native technologies is logical and promising.  The paper clearly outlines how technologies such as autoscaling, load balancing and migration can address these challenges.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper presents experimental results based on a Kubernetes cluster and the LLaMA-2 model which provide initial evidence to support claim that the cloud native approach can alleviate bottlenecks.
    *   **Clear and well written:** Paper is easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the application of Cloud Native principles to LLM inference serving is valuable, the core ideas are not entirely novel. Cloud Native concepts are established in other domains. The incremental contribution lies in applying them specifically to LLMs.
    *   **Experimental Scope:** The experimental setup, while reasonable, could be strengthened. Experiments focus on only one performance bottleneck, one cloud computing platform, one LLM model, and don't consider performance tradeoffs related to cloud native features.
    *   **Lack of Detailed Implementation Guidance:** The paper presents a high-level architecture but lacks detailed implementation specifics. Practical guidance on configuring autoscaling policies, optimizing inter-service communication, and profiling resource usage would enhance its practical value.
    *   **Limited Benchmarking:** The paper would benefit from comparing its Cloud Native approach against state-of-the-art inference serving systems (e.g., vLLM, TensorRT-LLM) to demonstrate its competitiveness.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper is focused on inference optimization and doesn't consider other aspects of the LLM lifecycle, such as training and fine-tuning.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper highlights the importance of Cloud Native technologies for LLM inference serving.
    *   It provides a framework for researchers and practitioners to explore Cloud Native solutions for optimizing LLM inference.
    *   It may inspire further research into developing more sophisticated and efficient Cloud Native LLM inference systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a sound application of existing concepts (Cloud Native) to a relevant and important problem (LLM inference). The paper provides a reasonable framework but has limited experimental depth, and a lack of comparison against existing inference serving frameworks. Given these factors, the paper demonstrates some value within the field, but needs further refinement to be considered a major contribution.

### GRR-CoCa: Leveraging LLM Mechanisms in Multimodal Model Architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18009v1)
- **Authors**: Jake R. Patock, Nicole Catherine Lewis, Kevin McCoy, Christina Gomez, Canling Chen, Lorenzo Luzi
- **Abstract**: State-of-the-art (SOTA) image and text generation models are multimodal models that have many similarities to large language models (LLMs). Despite achieving strong performances, leading foundational multimodal model architectures frequently lag behind the architectural sophistication of contemporary LLMs. We propose GRR-CoCa, an improved SOTA Contrastive Captioner (CoCa) model that incorporates Gaussian error gated linear units, root mean squared normalization, and rotary positional embedding into the textual decoders and the vision transformer (ViT) encoder. Each architectural modification has been shown to improve model performance in LLMs, but has yet to be adopted in CoCa. We benchmarked GRR-CoCa against Baseline CoCa, a model with the same modified textual decoders but with CoCa's original ViT encoder. We used standard pretraining and fine-tuning workflows to benchmark the models on contrastive and generative tasks. Our GRR-CoCa significantly outperformed Baseline CoCa on the pretraining dataset and three diverse fine-tuning datasets. Pretraining improvements were 27.25% in contrastive loss, 3.71% in perplexity, and 7.15% in CoCa loss. The average fine-tuning improvements were 13.66% in contrastive loss, 5.18% in perplexity, and 5.55% in CoCa loss. We show that GRR-CoCa's modified architecture improves performance and generalization across vision-language domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GRR-CoCa: Leveraging LLM Mechanisms in Multimodal Model Architectures":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRR-CoCa, an enhanced version of the Contrastive Captioner (CoCa) model, a state-of-the-art multimodal model for image and text generation. GRR-CoCa incorporates architectural improvements commonly found in modern Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically Gaussian Error Gated Linear Units (GEGLUs), Root Mean Squared Normalization (RMSNorm), and Rotary Positional Embedding (RoPE) into both the textual decoders and the vision transformer (ViT) encoder of CoCa.  The authors benchmark GRR-CoCa against a Baseline CoCa (with improved textual decoders but the original CoCa ViT encoder) using standard pretraining and fine-tuning workflows on various datasets. Results demonstrate significant performance improvements for GRR-CoCa in both contrastive and generative tasks, suggesting that integrating LLM architectural mechanisms enhances multimodal model performance and generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the application of LLM-inspired architectural improvements to *both* the text and vision components of the CoCa model. While GEGLUs, RMSNorm, and RoPE are individually known techniques, their concerted integration into a vision-language model *and* a thorough evaluation of their combined impact on the multimodal CoCa framework is relatively new. However, It's also important to acknowledge that simply applying known LLM architectures is incremental novelty, and whether these architectures are the most relevant or effective approach for vision transformers requires further examination.
*   **Significance:** The paper makes a practical contribution by demonstrating that architectural refinements from LLMs can be successfully transferred and scaled to multimodal models, improving their performance. It offers a potential pathway for enhancing existing vision-language models by incorporating readily available LLM techniques. This is significant because it potentially bridges the gap between advances in LLMs and related areas of multimodal learning, which previously was heavily reliant on textual architectures for downstream modelling tasks. The consistent improvement across multiple datasets strengthens the argument for the general applicability of the proposed modifications.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear experimental design with a well-defined Baseline CoCa for comparison.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation on multiple datasets representing diverse domains (general image captioning, medical image captioning, and benchmark datasets).
    *   Detailed descriptions of architectural modifications.
    *   Thorough exploration of hyperparameters and training techniques.
    *   Focuses on efficient and performance gains for model architectures.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper primarily focuses on *applying* existing LLM techniques to a multimodal model. While the results are positive, the analysis does not deeply explore *why* these specific architectural choices lead to improved performance. A more detailed investigation into the underlying mechanisms contributing to the observed gains would have strengthened the paper.
    *   While the authors attempt to maintain similar parameter sizes between the GRR-CoCa and the Baseline CoCa, a slight increase in the number of parameters is still notable. It would have been valuable to see the performance comparisons against models with the same parameter count.
    *   Lack of ablation studies. While the collective impact of GEGLUs, RoPE, and RMSNorm is clear, the specific contribution of each component needs to be explicitly addressed to demonstrate their impact.
    *   Could include metrics that are commonly used for captioning tasks (e.g. CIDEr, BLEU), to provide a common ground for the audience.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's influence depends on how widely the proposed modifications are adopted by researchers and practitioners. The relatively straightforward implementation and performance improvements suggest that GRR-CoCa could become a standard benchmark for future research in multimodal model architecture design. Additionally, the paper encourages further investigation into leveraging LLM techniques for vision-language models, and may spur research into other related architectural elements.
*   **Justification:** While the paper presents a useful application of known LLM techniques to improve the performance of a multimodal model, the level of novelty and depth of analysis are somewhat limited. The lack of explanation regarding *why* GEGLUs, RMSNorm, and RoPE improve performance, combined with the limited insights provided on parameter size and no ablation studies, reduces the impact of this work. However, it is a well executed paper, with a clear exposition on methods that could be taken on by many researchers, making it an important contribution to the research community.

### Direct Dual-Energy CT Material Decomposition using Model-based Denoising Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18012v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Xu, Alexandre Bousse, Alessandro Perelli
- **Abstract**: Dual-energy X-ray Computed Tomography (DECT) constitutes an advanced technology which enables automatic decomposition of materials in clinical images without manual segmentation using the dependency of the X-ray linear attenuation with energy. However, most methods perform material decomposition in the image domain as a post-processing step after reconstruction but this procedure does not account for the beam-hardening effect and it results in sub-optimal results. In this work, we propose a deep learning procedure called Dual-Energy Decomposition Model-based Diffusion (DEcomp-MoD) for quantitative material decomposition which directly converts the DECT projection data into material images. The algorithm is based on incorporating the knowledge of the spectral DECT model into the deep learning training loss and combining a score-based denoising diffusion learned prior in the material image domain. Importantly the inference optimization loss takes as inputs directly the sinogram and converts to material images through a model-based conditional diffusion model which guarantees consistency of the results. We evaluate the performance with both quantitative and qualitative estimation of the proposed DEcomp-MoD method on synthetic DECT sinograms from the low-dose AAPM dataset. Finally, we show that DEcomp-MoD outperform state-of-the-art unsupervised score-based model and supervised deep learning networks, with the potential to be deployed for clinical diagnosis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Direct Dual-Energy CT Material Decomposition using Model-based Denoising Diffusion Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel deep learning approach called DEcomp-MoD (Dual-Energy Decomposition Model-based Diffusion) for material decomposition in dual-energy computed tomography (DECT).  Unlike many existing methods that perform material decomposition as a post-processing step in the image domain, DEcomp-MoD directly converts DECT projection data (sinograms) into material images. The method integrates knowledge of the spectral DECT model into the deep learning training loss and combines a score-based denoising diffusion prior to improve the quality of the material image. Key features include model-based sinogram decomposition leveraging a U-Net, and a plug-and-play denoising diffusion prior to ensure result consistency. The authors evaluate the method on synthetic DECT data generated from the AAPM low-dose CT dataset, demonstrating improved performance over state-of-the-art unsupervised and supervised methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several elements into a cohesive framework for DECT material decomposition.  Specifically, this includes:
    *   A projection-based approach that operates directly on sinograms, aiming to circumvent the beam-hardening effects and error propagation issues of image-domain decomposition.
    *   The explicit use of the DECT forward model in the training loss function to ensure data consistency.
    *   Integration of a denoising diffusion model as an image prior within a plug-and-play framework.
    *   Model-based sinogram decomposition leveraging a U-Net.

    While each component individually might not be groundbreaking, the combination and application to the specific problem of DECT material decomposition is innovative. Similar diffusion model techniques exist but not specifically used with CT and material decomposition tasks in conjunction with the measurement model in the loss.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of the paper is in its ability to improve the accuracy and robustness of DECT material decomposition, particularly in low-dose scenarios or with sparse-view data.  Better material decomposition can lead to more accurate diagnoses, improved treatment planning, and enhanced capabilities in non-destructive testing. The experiments performed are also based on simulating synthetic low-dose scans which has more impact when deployed.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a relevant problem with clinical and industrial applications.
    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The integration of physical models and deep learning is a promising direction.
    *   The experimental results, while synthetic, demonstrate improvements over competing methods.
    *   The paper is reasonably well-written and technically sound.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on synthetic data is a major limitation. While the data is derived from real CT images, the simulation process introduces simplifying assumptions that may not fully reflect the complexities of real-world DECT data.  Real data would include more intricate noise patterns, calibration errors, and patient-specific variations.
    *   The computational cost is relatively high, even with optimizations like CG. More efficient iterative reconstruction would be of interest.
    *   The method requires separate training of the decomposition and diffusion models, which can be inefficient.
    *   No discussion of the hyperparameter sensitivity, which needs to be considered when deployed.

*   **Impact:** The immediate impact of the paper is somewhat limited by the synthetic data experiments.  However, the approach is promising and could have a more substantial impact if validated on real clinical DECT data.  The idea of combining model-based reconstruction with diffusion priors is likely to be influential in the field.

**Score:** 6.  While the paper presents a technically sound and novel approach, the heavy reliance on synthetic data limits its impact. The performance improvement is significant, and the combination of methods is novel but needs validation with experimental datasets.  If DEcomp-MoD is validated on real-world DECT data and demonstrates similar improvements, the significance would be notably higher. The code is not available for reproducibility. The computational complexity is high. The hyperparameter sensitivity requires further investigation. The innovation combines established deep learning with known measurement models but it is significant.

### Technical Report of TeleChat2, TeleChat2.5 and T1
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18013v1)
- **Authors**: Zihan Wang, Xinzhang Liu, Yitong Yao, Chao Wang, Yu Zhao, Zhihao Yang, Wenmin Deng, Kaipeng Jia, Jiaxin Peng, Yuyao Huang, Sishi Xiong, Zhuo Jiang, Kaidong Yu, Xiaohui Hu, Fubei Yao, Ruiyu Fang, Zhuoru Jiang, Ruiting Song, Qiyi Xie, Rui Xue, Xuewei He, Yanlei Xue, Zhu Yuan, Zhaoxi Zhang, Zilu Huang, Shiquan Wang, Xin Wang, Hanming Wu, Mingyuan Wang, Xufeng Zhan, Yuhan Sun, Zhaohu Xing, Yuhao Jiang, Bingkai Yang, Shuangyong Song, Yongxiang Li, Zhongjiang He, Xuelong Li
- **Abstract**: We introduce the latest series of TeleChat models: \textbf{TeleChat2}, \textbf{TeleChat2.5}, and \textbf{T1}, offering a significant upgrade over their predecessor, TeleChat. Despite minimal changes to the model architecture, the new series achieves substantial performance gains through enhanced training strategies in both pre-training and post-training stages. The series begins with \textbf{TeleChat2}, which undergoes pretraining on 10 trillion high-quality and diverse tokens. This is followed by Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to further enhance its capabilities. \textbf{TeleChat2.5} and \textbf{T1} expand the pipeline by incorporating a continual pretraining phase with domain-specific datasets, combined with reinforcement learning (RL) to improve performance in code generation and mathematical reasoning tasks. The \textbf{T1} variant is designed for complex reasoning, supporting long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning and demonstrating substantial improvements in mathematics and coding. In contrast, \textbf{TeleChat2.5} prioritizes speed, delivering rapid inference. Both flagship models of \textbf{T1} and \textbf{TeleChat2.5} are dense Transformer-based architectures with 115B parameters, showcasing significant advancements in reasoning and general task performance compared to the original TeleChat. Notably, \textbf{T1-115B} outperform proprietary models such as OpenAI's o1-mini and GPT-4o. We publicly release \textbf{TeleChat2}, \textbf{TeleChat2.5} and \textbf{T1}, including post-trained versions with 35B and 115B parameters, to empower developers and researchers with state-of-the-art language models tailored for diverse applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided technical report:

**Summary:**

The technical report introduces TeleChat2, TeleChat2.5, and T1, a new series of large language models (LLMs) developed by TeleAI.  These models build upon the previous TeleChat architecture but achieve significant performance gains through enhanced training strategies, including pre-training on a much larger dataset (10 trillion tokens), long-context annealing, and post-training via Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Reinforcement Learning (RL). The report highlights improvements in reasoning, coding, tool use, and instruction following.  Notably, T1 is designed for complex reasoning and long Chain-of-Thought (CoT), while TeleChat2.5 prioritizes speed.  The models are released in 35B and 115B parameter versions, with the T1-115B model reportedly outperforming some proprietary models. The report details the architecture, training process (data curation, cleaning, training details, engineering infrastructure), and evaluation results across a range of benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale and Scope:** The report documents a significant effort in training large language models, detailing the training data, model architecture, and infrastructure. The sheer scale of pre-training (10 trillion tokens) and model size (115B parameters) are notable.
    *   **Comprehensive Training Methodology:** The training pipeline incorporates state-of-the-art techniques like SFT, DPO, and RL, addressing key challenges in LLM development such as alignment and reasoning. The detailed explanation of data curation and cleaning processes is valuable.
    *   **Long-Context Handling:** The long-context annealing stage, with extensions to 128K and 256K tokens, directly addresses a major limitation of many LLMs and is crucial for real-world applications. The techniques for long-sequence optimization are insightful.
    *   **Performance:** The reported results indicate substantial improvements over the previous TeleChat and, in some cases, outperforming other models, including proprietary ones (though these comparisons need careful scrutiny).
    *   **Open Source Contribution:** Releasing the models and code promotes further research and development within the community. The inclusion of tools for fine-tuning and deployment is particularly useful.
    *   **Detailed Engineering:** The report details the hardware and software infrastructure (MindSpore, Atlas 900 A2 cluster), including parallelism strategies and optimizations. The descriptions of failure mitigation strategies are also a key strength.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Architecture:** The report states that the architecture is largely unchanged from the previous TeleChat. This implies that the primary innovation lies in the training methodology and scaling rather than architectural breakthroughs.
    *   **Benchmark Transparency:** While a variety of benchmarks are listed, the exact prompts, evaluation protocols, and comparison details are limited.  A more rigorous and transparent comparison with other open-source and closed-source models (beyond simply listing some higher scores) would significantly strengthen the evaluation.  For instance, more details on the error bars or confidence intervals of the metrics in the evaluation tables should be included, to allow for a sounder comparison between different results.
    *   **Downstream Task Focus:** The report primarily focuses on benchmarking general knowledge and reasoning capabilities. There is less emphasis on specialized capabilities or novel applications demonstrated using these models.
    *   **Lack of Ablation studies:** The report would benefit from ablation studies to show the specific impacts of the various components (long-context training, RL training).
    *   **Limited Details on Synthetic Data Generation:** While the paper mentions the use of synthetic queries, especially for mathematics and coding, the methods and prompt engineering used for this generation could use more detail and validation in terms of their quality.
    *   **Hardware Specificity:** The reliance on MindSpore and the Ascend NPU architecture might limit accessibility and reproducibility for some researchers.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The release contributes to the growing ecosystem of open-source LLMs.
    *   The long-context capabilities are valuable for applications requiring extensive reasoning.
    *   The detailed training methodology provides insights for training other large models.
    *   The model family offers options for different computational resource constraints.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

The report documents a substantial engineering effort and presents impressive results. The long-context handling and comprehensive training pipeline are particularly noteworthy. However, the lack of architectural novelty, limited benchmark transparency, and the reliance on specific hardware configurations temper the overall assessment. A more thorough comparative analysis against existing LLMs, more detailed analysis (e.g. ablation studies), and less hardware-specific implementation would significantly improve the paper. As it stands, the report is a valuable contribution to the open-source LLM landscape but does not represent a paradigm shift in LLM architecture or training.

### Predictive Scaling Laws for Efficient GRPO Training of Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18014v1)
- **Authors**: Datta Nimmaturi, Vaishnavi Bhargava, Rajat Ghosh, Johnu George, Debojyoti Dutta
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) for reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is computationally expensive. To address this, we propose a predictive framework that models training dynamics and helps optimize resource usage. Through experiments on Llama and Qwen models (3B 8B), we derive an empirical scaling law based on model size, initial performance, and training progress. This law predicts reward trajectories and identifies three consistent training phases: slow start, rapid improvement, and plateau. We find that training beyond certain number of an epoch offers little gain, suggesting earlier stopping can significantly reduce compute without sacrificing performance. Our approach generalizes across model types, providing a practical guide for efficient GRPO-based fine-tuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Predictive Scaling Laws for Efficient GRPO Training of Large Reasoning Models" focuses on improving the efficiency of fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) for complex reasoning tasks, specifically using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The authors develop a predictive framework to understand and optimize the GRPO training process. They conduct experiments across different model architectures (Llama and Qwen models of varying sizes) and derive empirical scaling laws that predict model reward trajectories during GRPO training.  The scaling law incorporates model size, initial performance, and training progression to predict performance plateaus and convergence. The results show sigmoid-shaped learning curves with distinct phases: slow initial progress, rapid improvement, and plateau. A key finding is that training beyond the convergence point yields minimal gains, which allows for informed resource allocation decisions. They utilize parameter-efficient fine-tuning (LoRA) and quantization to make the training more accessible. The work aims to provide a quantitative framework for optimizing GRPO training in reasoning tasks, avoiding wasteful over-training while maintaining model quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in deriving and validating empirical scaling laws specifically for GRPO training of LLMs on reasoning tasks. While scaling laws are not entirely new in the context of LLMs, their application and customization to GRPO, combined with the inclusion of factors like initial performance and training progression, demonstrate a valuable refinement. The study contributes to more efficient fine-tuning strategies for reasoning-capable models, a key area of LLM research. However, the application of LoRA and quantization is fairly standard practice within the field, diminishing the overall novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: the high computational cost of fine-tuning LLMs for complex reasoning. By predicting performance plateaus and enabling informed stopping criteria, the work has the potential to reduce computational waste and make these models more accessible to researchers with limited resources. The findings provide practical guidelines for optimizing GRPO training, potentially accelerating research in reasoning-capable LLMs. The generalizability across different model sizes and architectures further enhances the impact. Furthermore, their detailed explanation is aimed to assist in reproducing their work and thus increases the significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive experimentation:**  The experiments are well-designed, spanning multiple model architectures and sizes.
    *   **Clear scaling laws:** The derivation and validation of the scaling laws are clearly presented.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper directly addresses practical concerns by providing actionable insights for resource allocation and training duration.
    *   **Reproducibility emphasis**: The paper emphasizes how to make their work reproducible using publicly available datasets and open source tools.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited dataset:** The reliance on a single dataset (OpenThoughts) raises concerns about the generalizability of the scaling laws to other reasoning tasks or datasets.
    *   **Model scope:** While the study considers different model architectures, the selection of Llama and Qwen models limits the scope. It's unclear how these scaling laws apply to other LLM families.
    *   **Parameter tuning:**  The results may be sensitive to specific hyperparameter settings. The paper could benefit from discussing the impact of varying these settings and providing guidance on parameter tuning.
    *   **Incremental improvements**: They do not compare to existing literature regarding scaling models to determine how big of an improvement it has compared to other research.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper can influence the community by providing a tangible framework for efficiently training LLMs for reasoning tasks with GRPO. Researchers and practitioners can use the derived scaling laws to make more informed decisions about resource allocation and training schedules. This work could inspire further research on adapting and improving scaling laws for other reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning techniques and diverse reasoning tasks.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM fine-tuning by developing and validating scaling laws specifically for GRPO-based reasoning. The focus on resource efficiency and practical implications strengthens its relevance. However, the limitations regarding dataset generalizability, model scope, and the incremental novelty compared to existing literature on scaling laws keep the score from being higher.  The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, making this a worthwhile contribution, but not a groundbreaking one.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### NeuralDB: Scaling Knowledge Editing in LLMs to 100,000 Facts with Neural KV Database
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18028v1)
- **Authors**: Weizhi Fei, Hao Shi, Jing Xu, Jingchen Peng, Jiazheng Li, Jingzhao Zhang, Bo Bai, Wei Han, Zhenyuan Chen, Xueyan Niu
- **Abstract**: Efficiently editing knowledge stored in large language models (LLMs) enables model updates without large-scale training. One possible solution is Locate-and-Edit (L\&E), allowing simultaneous modifications of a massive number of facts. However, such editing may compromise the general abilities of LLMs and even result in forgetting edited facts when scaling up to thousands of edits. In this paper, we model existing linear L\&E methods as querying a Key-Value (KV) database. From this perspective, we then propose NeuralDB, an editing framework that explicitly represents the edited facts as a neural KV database equipped with a non-linear gated retrieval module, % In particular, our gated module only operates when inference involves the edited facts, effectively preserving the general abilities of LLMs. Comprehensive experiments involving the editing of 10,000 facts were conducted on the ZsRE and CounterFacts datasets, using GPT2-XL, GPT-J (6B) and Llama-3 (8B). The results demonstrate that NeuralDB not only excels in editing efficacy, generalization, specificity, fluency, and consistency, but also preserves overall performance across six representative text understanding and generation tasks. Further experiments indicate that NeuralDB maintains its effectiveness even when scaled to 100,000 facts (\textbf{50x} more than in prior work).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NeuralDB, a novel framework for scaling knowledge editing (KE) in large language models (LLMs).  The core idea is to explicitly represent edited facts as a neural Key-Value (KV) database integrated into the LLM's feedforward network (FFN) layers using a non-linear gated retrieval module. This approach overcomes the limitations of existing Locate-and-Edit (L&E) methods, which struggle to maintain general abilities and avoid forgetting edited facts when scaling to thousands of edits. The framework allows for efficient editing, preserving general knowledge, and easy manipulation of the database (appending, modifying, deleting facts).  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of NeuralDB through experiments involving editing up to 100,000 facts on various models (GPT-2 XL, GPT-J, and Llama-3), showing improved editing efficacy, generalization, specificity, fluency, and consistency, while preserving overall performance on text understanding and generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in reframing existing L&E methods through the lens of a KV database and then proposing a concrete neural KV database implementation. While the idea of using external knowledge stores isn't entirely new in the broader LLM literature, the specific application to knowledge editing and the architectural design of the gated retrieval module integrated directly within the FFN layer is a significant contribution.

The non-linear gated retrieval function is crucial for the method's success. The paper provides empirical evidence suggesting that linear L&E methods, when scaled up, often struggle to selectively activate edited knowledge without interfering with other knowledge in the model. NeuralDB's gating mechanism allows for a more precise selection of the relevant residual vector, leading to better performance.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from addressing a key challenge in LLM maintenance and customization: efficiently updating knowledge without compromising general abilities.  The ability to edit tens of thousands of facts (and potentially more) with minimal performance degradation has practical implications for keeping LLMs up-to-date and adapting them to specific domains. The demonstration of scaling to 100,000 facts is a significant advancement over prior work.

The results are compelling, showing consistent improvements across multiple models and datasets.  The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of various design choices, such as the layer selection and the gating parameter.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing KE methods, particularly when scaling to a large number of edits.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The KV database analogy provides a solid foundation for the proposed framework.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering a wide range of tasks, models, and datasets.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper includes ablation studies and visualizations to support the claims.
*   **Scalability:** The demonstration of scaling to 100,000 facts is a major achievement.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide sufficient details about their implementation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions memory usage and computation time, a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead of NeuralDB, particularly during training and inference, would be beneficial. The increased inference time especially is critical.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The ablation study shows the performance is sensitive to the setting of the gating parameter gamma.  The paper does not provide guidelines on how to best choose this parameter for other models or datasets. More in-depth analysis would be welcome.
*   **Limited Scope of Knowledge:** The paper focuses primarily on factual knowledge editing. It would be interesting to explore the applicability of NeuralDB to other types of knowledge, such as reasoning rules or stylistic preferences.

**Potential Influence:**

NeuralDB has the potential to influence future research in knowledge editing and LLM customization. The framework's scalability, modularity, and ease of deployment make it a promising approach for building trustworthy and adaptable LLMs. The KV database analogy could inspire new architectures and algorithms for managing knowledge in LLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of knowledge editing for LLMs. Its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. It's not a groundbreaking, paradigm-shifting paper that would revolutionize LLMs (which would merit a 9 or 10). Instead, it presents a technically sound, well-evaluated, and practically relevant solution to a key problem. The work clearly advances the state-of-the-art by enabling scaling to 100,000 edited facts while preserving model capabilities. The reframing of the L&E paradigm, coupled with the gated neural KV database implementation, represents a meaningful contribution to the field, but the method's long-term scalability and hyperparameter sensitivity are things to note.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ViGText: Deepfake Image Detection with Vision-Language Model Explanations and Graph Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18031v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmad ALBarqawi, Mahmoud Nazzal, Issa Khalil, Abdallah Khreishah, NhatHai Phan
- **Abstract**: The rapid rise of deepfake technology, which produces realistic but fraudulent digital content, threatens the authenticity of media. Traditional deepfake detection approaches often struggle with sophisticated, customized deepfakes, especially in terms of generalization and robustness against malicious attacks. This paper introduces ViGText, a novel approach that integrates images with Vision Large Language Model (VLLM) Text explanations within a Graph-based framework to improve deepfake detection. The novelty of ViGText lies in its integration of detailed explanations with visual data, as it provides a more context-aware analysis than captions, which often lack specificity and fail to reveal subtle inconsistencies. ViGText systematically divides images into patches, constructs image and text graphs, and integrates them for analysis using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to identify deepfakes. Through the use of multi-level feature extraction across spatial and frequency domains, ViGText captures details that enhance its robustness and accuracy to detect sophisticated deepfakes. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ViGText significantly enhances generalization and achieves a notable performance boost when it detects user-customized deepfakes. Specifically, average F1 scores rise from 72.45% to 98.32% under generalization evaluation, and reflects the model's superior ability to generalize to unseen, fine-tuned variations of stable diffusion models. As for robustness, ViGText achieves an increase of 11.1% in recall compared to other deepfake detection approaches. When facing targeted attacks that exploit its graph-based architecture, ViGText limits classification performance degradation to less than 4%. ViGText uses detailed visual and textual analysis to set a new standard for detecting deepfakes, helping ensure media authenticity and information integrity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ViGText: Deepfake Image Detection with Vision-Language Model Explanations and Graph Neural Networks":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViGText, a novel approach for deepfake image detection that integrates visual analysis with textual explanations generated by Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) within a graph-based framework. ViGText divides images into patches, constructs image and text graphs, and uses Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to analyze these graphs and detect inconsistencies. Key innovations include using detailed VLLM explanations instead of simple captions, incorporating spatial and frequency domain features, and using a dual-graph structure for analysis. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ViGText enhances generalization, improves robustness against adversarial attacks, and achieves state-of-the-art detection performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of ViGText lies in its specific combination of existing techniques, rather than inventing entirely new components. Using graphs for image analysis, VLLMs for explanation, and GNNs for classification are individually known. However, the synergistic integration of detailed textual explanations with visual data within a graph-based model is a valuable approach. This specific architecture, which uses VLLMs not just for captions, but for localized patch-level explanations, represents a significant advancement compared to prior methods such as DE-FAKE.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical and timely problem: the detection of increasingly sophisticated deepfakes. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements in generalization to user-customized models and robustness against adversarial attacks. The results are particularly strong in the generalization experiments across fine-tuned variants of Stable Diffusion. The focus on robustness, especially against adaptive adversaries leveraging foundation models, is highly relevant in the evolving landscape of deepfake technology. The improved performance and the thorough evaluation on a challenging and diverse dataset (SD, StyleCLIP, and fine-tuned variants) highlights the significant contribution of this work to the deepfake detection community. The approach has practical significance, providing a framework that can be deployed to preserve media authenticity.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dual-graph framework provides a robust method for integrating and analyzing visual and textual information.
    *   Detailed textual explanations improve context-aware analysis and expose subtle inconsistencies in deepfakes.
    *   The use of spatial and frequency domain features enhances detection robustness.
    *   The paper provides extensive experimental results demonstrating significant improvements in generalization and robustness compared to existing methods.
    *   The evaluation addresses real-world challenges, particularly user-customized models and foundation-model-based adversarial attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on a VLLM, so performance is tied to the capabilities of the underlying VLLM and its susceptibility to biases or manipulations. It may be vulnerable to certain types of targeted attacks aimed at manipulating the VLLM's output, even if indirectly. The limited number of patch-level textual examples could create challenges in graph-based integration.
    *   The work focuses primarily on image-based deepfakes; extending the approach to audio or video requires addressing temporal dynamics and synchronization.
    *   The paper acknowledges, but does not solve for, white-box attacks that jointly optimize both the image generator and VLLM to evade detection. This is a significant limitation.

*   **Potential Influence:** ViGText has the potential to significantly influence the field of deepfake detection. Its novel framework provides a valuable foundation for future research, including:
    *   Developing adaptive patching strategies to optimize patch size based on image content.
    *   Exploring heterogeneous graph neural networks to improve feature representation.
    *   Extending the approach to other modalities, such as audio and video.
    *   Investigating defenses against adversarial attacks targeting both the image and the VLLM.
    *   Adaptive linking mechanisms between image patches and text explanations.

**Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the deepfake detection field, presenting a novel and effective framework that addresses key limitations in existing methods. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating improved generalization and robustness. While the method has some limitations and potential vulnerabilities, it provides a valuable foundation for future research.
Taking into account the strengths and weaknesses described above, here is my proposed score for the contribution:

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** ViGText offers a tangible improvement over existing deepfake detection methods, especially in addressing generalization and adversarial robustness. Its primary strength is in its architectural innovation, carefully integrating diverse elements to achieve a synergistic effect that surpasses its individual components. The thorough experimental validation convincingly demonstrates the system's efficacy. While the method relies on VLLMs and doesn't fully address white-box attacks, the advancements and potential for future research justify a rating of 7.5 for the study's novelty and significance.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### OpenNav: Open-World Navigation with Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18033v1)
- **Authors**: Mingfeng Yuan, Letian Wang, Steven L. Waslander
- **Abstract**: Pre-trained large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong common-sense reasoning abilities, making them promising for robotic navigation and planning tasks. However, despite recent progress, bridging the gap between language descriptions and actual robot actions in the open-world, beyond merely invoking limited predefined motion primitives, remains an open challenge. In this work, we aim to enable robots to interpret and decompose complex language instructions, ultimately synthesizing a sequence of trajectory points to complete diverse navigation tasks given open-set instructions and open-set objects. We observe that multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) exhibit strong cross-modal understanding when processing free-form language instructions, demonstrating robust scene comprehension. More importantly, leveraging their code-generation capability, MLLMs can interact with vision-language perception models to generate compositional 2D bird-eye-view value maps, effectively integrating semantic knowledge from MLLMs with spatial information from maps to reinforce the robot's spatial understanding. To further validate our approach, we effectively leverage large-scale autonomous vehicle datasets (AVDs) to validate our proposed zero-shot vision-language navigation framework in outdoor navigation tasks, demonstrating its capability to execute a diverse range of free-form natural language navigation instructions while maintaining robustness against object detection errors and linguistic ambiguities. Furthermore, we validate our system on a Husky robot in both indoor and outdoor scenes, demonstrating its real-world robustness and applicability. Supplementary videos are available at https://trailab.github.io/OpenNav-website/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the OpenNav paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents OpenNav, a novel zero-shot Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) framework that enables robots to navigate open-world environments based on free-form language instructions, without relying on pre-trained skills, motion primitives, or in-context examples. OpenNav leverages the reasoning, code generation, and planning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in conjunction with an open-vocabulary perception system (OVPS) to generate dense, geometry-compliant trajectories. The framework utilizes a task-agnostic prompt and a multimodal interface to enhance robustness against uncertainties in open-set objects and language. The authors validate OpenNav through experiments on autonomous vehicle datasets (AVDs) and a ground robot, demonstrating its ability to execute diverse navigation tasks while maintaining robustness against object detection errors and linguistic ambiguities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *direct generation of trajectories by an MLLM for outdoor navigation, operating in a true zero-shot manner*, without relying on motion primitives, pre-trained skills, or in-context examples.  While existing works utilize LLMs for high-level planning or object detection, OpenNav attempts a much deeper integration for trajectory synthesis.  The specific integration of a multi-expert system linking the MLLM with an OVPS to ground instructions spatially is a clear contribution. Furthermore, the approach of generating trajectory code segment by segment and debugging is also potentially interesting.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in robotics: bridging the gap between high-level language commands and low-level robot actions in unstructured, open-world environments. If the framework proves robust and generalizable, it could significantly improve human-robot interaction and the autonomy of robots in real-world tasks.  The use of AVDs for validation is also a strength, making the results directly relevant to outdoor navigation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Zero-Shot Capability:**  The claim of zero-shot capability is a significant advantage, potentially simplifying deployment and adaptation to new environments.
    *   **Multimodal Integration:** The integrated design of the MLLM and OVPS system, combining visual and textual information, seems effective in resolving ambiguities and improving robustness.
    *   **AVD Validation:** The use of autonomous vehicle datasets allows the proposed technique to be validated in realistic complex outdoor environments.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on ChatGPT-4O:** The reliance on ChatGPT-4O, a proprietary model, limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the research.  Ideally, a more open-source approach or ablation studies with other models would be beneficial. The paper presents it as a given that ChatGPT-4O performs best.
    *   **Limited Quantitative Evaluation on Real Robots:** While a Husky robot is mentioned, the details and quantitative results from real-world experiments are not adequately elaborated.  The tables focus heavily on AVDs.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While an ablation study comparing trajectory generation methods is included, it is not comprehensive. Removing certain modules of the proposed technique would better validate their contributions.
    *   **Generality/Overclaiming:** Although the paper makes broad claims of generalizability, the experiments are limited to somewhat similar scenarios and tasks. The true performance in drastically different open-world environments remains an open question. The "human-like coarse trajectory" is probably a bit too strong a statement.
    *   **Implementation Details:** The paper could benefit from more detail on the implementation, particularly regarding the prompts used with the MLLM. This is important to assess the replicability and generalizability of the approach.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in VLN, human-robot interaction, and the application of LLMs to robotics. It may encourage further exploration of direct trajectory generation from MLLMs and the development of more robust and integrated perception-planning systems.
**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially valuable contribution to the field of VLN. The zero-shot trajectory generation using MLLMs is a novel idea, and the multimodal integration strategy seems promising. However, the significant reliance on a proprietary model, limitations in the experimental validation (especially regarding real-world robot deployments), and questions about the overgeneralized claims of robustness and performance merit a conservative score. Furthermore, while the integration seems strong, the "black box" nature of the solution is likely to raise concerns in safety-critical applications. A higher score would have required a more thorough evaluation, including comparative results with other state-of-the-art methods and more extensive real-world testing. The reliance on a black box tool weakens the impact and reproducibility of the research.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Removing Box-Free Watermarks for Image-to-Image Models via Query-Based Reverse Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18034v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan An, Guang Hua, Hangcheng Cao, Zhengru Fang, Guowen Xu, Susanto Rahardja, Yuguang Fang
- **Abstract**: The intellectual property of deep generative networks (GNets) can be protected using a cascaded hiding network (HNet) which embeds watermarks (or marks) into GNet outputs, known as box-free watermarking. Although both GNet and HNet are encapsulated in a black box (called operation network, or ONet), with only the generated and marked outputs from HNet being released to end users and deemed secure, in this paper, we reveal an overlooked vulnerability in such systems. Specifically, we show that the hidden GNet outputs can still be reliably estimated via query-based reverse engineering, leaking the generated and unmarked images, despite the attacker's limited knowledge of the system. Our first attempt is to reverse-engineer an inverse model for HNet under the stringent black-box condition, for which we propose to exploit the query process with specially curated input images. While effective, this method yields unsatisfactory image quality. To improve this, we subsequently propose an alternative method leveraging the equivalent additive property of box-free model watermarking and reverse-engineering a forward surrogate model of HNet, with better image quality preservation. Extensive experimental results on image processing and image generation tasks demonstrate that both attacks achieve impressive watermark removal success rates (100%) while also maintaining excellent image quality (reaching the highest PSNR of 34.69 dB), substantially outperforming existing attacks, highlighting the urgent need for robust defensive strategies to mitigate the identified vulnerability in box-free model watermarking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of box-free watermarking schemes used to protect deep generative networks (GNets). These schemes embed watermarks into the outputs of GNets using a cascaded hiding network (HNet), with both networks encapsulated in a black box operation network (ONet). The paper reveals that by crafting specific queries and using reverse engineering techniques, an attacker can estimate the hidden outputs of the GNet, effectively removing the watermark and exposing the original generated images. The paper proposes two attack methods: first, reverse engineering an inverse model of HNet; and second, exploiting the additive property of the watermark embedding to directly estimate the GNet output. The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed attacks in removing watermarks while maintaining good image quality. The paper also discusses potential defense mechanisms and highlights the need for more robust defenses against query-based reverse engineering attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in identifying and demonstrating a previously overlooked vulnerability in box-free watermarking schemes for image-to-image models. While box-free watermarking was considered relatively secure, this paper effectively demonstrates how, with carefully designed queries, the protection afforded by the black-box nature of ONet can be circumvented. The exploitation of the additive property of watermarking in the second attack is a clever insight that leads to improved image quality after watermark removal. The second attack presents a higher degree of novelty when compared to the first, as it introduces a specific methodology for exploiting this property to approximate the GNet's output.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is substantial. It shows that existing box-free watermarking techniques are less secure than previously thought, particularly against attackers who can make multiple queries to the system. This finding has important implications for protecting the intellectual property of DNN models deployed as cloud services. The vulnerability discovery calls for a re-evaluation of existing defensive strategies and the development of new countermeasures against reverse engineering attacks. The paper also offers a concrete approach for breaking a previously assumed secure system, which can help in designing more robust protection mechanisms. The results directly impact the security analysis of AI-as-a-service platforms, highlighting the risks associated with seemingly secure, black-box models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and threat model.
    *   Well-defined attack methodologies with clear explanations.
    *   Exploitation of the additive property of box-free watermarking for effective and high-quality watermark removal.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed attacks.
    *   Discussion of potential defensive strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The first attack, while intuitive, may be considered a more straightforward application of reverse engineering. The resulting image quality isn't great, but it helps motivate the second attack.
    *   Although mentioned briefly, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis and experimental validation of the proposed API detection defense mechanism. It lacks enough detail for immediate implementation.
    *   The paper assumes a relatively powerful attacker who can make many queries to the ONet. The practicality of this assumption should be discussed in the context of real-world API usage and rate limiting.
    *   The defense strategy mentioned is not very robust and could easily be circumvented by an attacker with a basic understanding of the methodology.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can be expected to influence research in the area of DNN model watermarking and protection. It should encourage the development of more robust watermarking schemes that are resilient to query-based reverse engineering attacks. It might also stimulate work on better API security measures for protecting cloud-based DNN services, including input filtering, access control, and anomaly detection. The work may encourage further research in extracting private information from black-box AI models through query-based techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. While the core concept of leveraging the additive property of the watermarking method is a clever and insightful idea that enables effective watermark removal with minimal image degradation, the study also contains a few limitations. The work reveals an important vulnerability in a widely adopted watermarking scheme, demonstrating that the black-box security of ONet can be breached. The second attack effectively uses forward modeling instead of reverse modeling, showing how to exploit an underlying characteristic of box-free watermarking. The experimental section also demonstrates that the attack is successful under more realistic conditions when compared to the related works. However, the defense method described could be improved to improve its robustness. The paper also doesn’t explore how well the proposed approach would work with different underlying generative networks (e.g., diffusion based, GAN based, etc.). While the paper's novelty is strong, the limitations warrant a lower score than a 8 or 9. A score lower than 7 is not justified, given that the findings have significant implications for the security of deployed AI models.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### NWaaS: Nonintrusive Watermarking as a Service for X-to-Image DNN
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18036v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan An, Guang Hua, Yu Guo, Hangcheng Cao, Susanto Rahardja, Yuguang Fang
- **Abstract**: The intellectual property of deep neural network (DNN) models can be protected with DNN watermarking, which embeds copyright watermarks into model parameters (white-box), model behavior (black-box), or model outputs (box-free), and the watermarks can be subsequently extracted to verify model ownership or detect model theft. Despite recent advances, these existing methods are inherently intrusive, as they either modify the model parameters or alter the structure. This natural intrusiveness raises concerns about watermarking-induced shifts in model behavior and the additional cost of fine-tuning, further exacerbated by the rapidly growing model size. As a result, model owners are often reluctant to adopt DNN watermarking in practice, which limits the development of practical Watermarking as a Service (WaaS) systems. To address this issue, we introduce Nonintrusive Watermarking as a Service (NWaaS), a novel trustless paradigm designed for X-to-Image models, in which we hypothesize that with the model untouched, an owner-defined watermark can still be extracted from model outputs. Building on this concept, we propose ShadowMark, a concrete implementation of NWaaS which addresses critical deployment challenges by establishing a robust and nonintrusive side channel in the protected model's black-box API, leveraging a key encoder and a watermark decoder. It is significantly distinctive from existing solutions by attaining the so-called absolute fidelity and being applicable to different DNN architectures, while being also robust against existing attacks, eliminating the fidelity-robustness trade-off. Extensive experiments on image-to-image, noise-to-image, noise-and-text-to-image, and text-to-image models, demonstrate the efficacy and practicality of ShadowMark for real-world deployment of nonintrusive DNN watermarking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Nonintrusive Watermarking as a Service (NWaaS), a new paradigm for DNN watermarking, specifically for X-to-Image models.  NWaaS aims to address the limitations of existing DNN watermarking methods (white-box, black-box, and box-free) by eliminating the need to modify model parameters or architecture.
